身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元大銀行所有，非經本行同
意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕不另行通知。
金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 9 月 21 日
上週重點訊息
◼ Fed 三年來首次升息，點陣圖顯示年底前再升一碼：Fed 於 9/16 一致決議升息 1 碼，將聯
邦基金利率目標區間提高至 3.75%～4.00%，為 2023 年 7 月以來首次升息。最新利率預測
中，18 名官員有 16 人預期年底前至少仍需再升息 1 碼。最新經濟預測顯示，Fed 上修今明
兩年實質 GDP 成長率並下修失業率預估值，但也預期通膨壓力將比原先估計更為頑強。主席
Warsh 並指出美國經濟、就業、內需與資本投資仍具韌性，金融環境難稱緊縮。

◼ AI 安全疑慮引發半導體股急跌，但科技產業內部仍呈現明顯分化： OpenAI、Anthropic 等
AI 業者高層呼籲放慢 AI 發展速度、強化安全防護，引發市場重新評估 AI 投資成長速度與資
本支出前景，拖累費城半導體指數 9/14 重挫約 5.9%，但軟體股反而上漲。現階段尚不足以
證明 AI 資本支出循環已反轉，資金亦未全面撤離科技產業，而是從估值較高、籌碼較擁擠的
AI 硬體轉向其他科技次產業。
◼ 日本央行決議升息 1 碼，符合市場預期：日本央行 9/18 以 7 比 2 決議將無擔保隔夜拆款利
率由 1.0%調升至 1.25%。此為該行 2024 年來第 6 次升息，符合市場預期，故升息後對風險
資產衝擊有限。BOJ 明言將「繼續升息並調整寬鬆程度」 ，並將審視調整的時點與步調。
市場展望與操作策略
◼ 科技股中期趨勢未見反轉，但短線波動料加劇：儘管 FOMC 9 月升息，點陣圖暗示今年可能
再升一碼，但其對經濟成長率與失業率預期仍朝正向發展，同時股市也止跌上揚，顯示投資

人認為企業獲利成長動能足以抵消利率上揚的負面影響，基本面較佳的科技股仍具資金承
接，惟宜留意股市短線仍可能因地緣政治與經濟數據表現而出現波動。
◼ 中天期美債及投資級債風險報酬相對均衡：Fed 重啟升息且點陣圖暗示年底前或再升息，短天
期公債殖利率直接受到政策利率上修影響 ； 天期過長的債券則持續面臨財政赤字 、 發債供給及
期限溢價上升壓力，利率敏感度偏高。中天期(5~10 年期)美國公債既可取得目前相對較高收
益，亦具適度存續期間，在未來通膨降溫時較有機會受惠於殖利率回落，風險報酬較為均衡。
投資級債亦具較高收益與信用防禦特性，有助平衡投資組合波動。

## Document 025: 20261005_投資週報_製造業加速擴張，AI需求推動市場

Report source: Yuanta (投資週報)
Date: 2026-10-05
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本
身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元大銀行所有，非經本行同
意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕不另行通知。
金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 10 月 05 日
上週重點訊息
◼ 美國製造業維持擴張，成本壓力升高：9 月 ISM 製造業調查顯示，美國製造業連續第九個月
位於擴張區間，新訂單與就業分項均見改善，反映接單與用人需求仍有支撐。然而，價格指數
由 8 月的 71.1 升至 9 月的 77.9，能源、金屬及關稅相關成本上升，加上供應商交貨偏慢，企
業面臨更大成本壓力 。 後續須觀察企業能否轉嫁成本 ， 以及通膨黏性對聯準會政策判斷的影響 。
◼ 日本短觀顯示製造業改善、服務業略緩：日本銀行 10 月 1 日公布的 9 月短觀顯示，大型製造

業景氣判斷指數由 6 月的＋22 升至＋24，大型非製造業則由＋37 降至＋35。企業預估 12 月
兩項指數分別回落至＋21 與＋30，顯示現況仍有支撐，後續看法則偏審慎。製造業改善有助
支撐日股獲利預期，但服務業放緩及日銀政策路徑仍須持續觀察。
◼ 台灣製造業活動加速擴張，AI 需求續強：10 月 1 日公布的製造業調查顯示，台灣 9 月製造業
活動擴張速度創 2021 年 8 月以來最快，AI 相關應用需求仍是重要推力。國發會 9 月 29 日公
布的 8 月景氣對策信號續呈紅燈，綜合判斷分數為 41 分，與 7 月持平，領先及同時指標皆續
升。科技供應鏈景氣仍有支撐，但成長動能能否擴及其他產業，仍是觀察後續景氣的重點。
市場展望
◼ AI 需求持續擴張，關注財報季表現： AI 基礎建設需求未受利率環境影響，而是持續擴張，帶
動光通訊、CCL、ABF 載板 、記憶體、先進製程與封裝等關鍵供應鏈維持強勁成長，市場表
現由全面上漲轉向結構性分化。隨新一代 AI 伺服器放量、CSP 資本支出維持高檔，具供給優

勢、定價能力及訂單能見度延伸至 2027 年的龍頭廠商仍可望受惠。10 月中旬起陸續公布的
第三季財報，若結果持續表現亮眼，可望為市場再添動能，屆時具 AI 供應鏈題材的台股與日
股亦可望受惠。
◼ 債券佈局聚焦 5~10 年期美國公債及投等債：雖然貨幣政策風險 下降有助於穩定長期通膨預
期，但政府與企業發債供給增加所帶來的排擠效應，以及美國經濟成長展現韌性，仍可能使長
天期利率面臨上行壓力。因此，在當前利率環境下，5~10 年期債券較能兼顧票息收益與存續
期間風險管理。投資級債方面，彭博美國投資級公司債指數利差約 80-90 個基點，雖然利差
偏窄，但高基準利率環境仍提供具吸引力的絕對收益水準，因此現階段5~10 年期美國公債及
投資級債的風險報酬結構相對較佳。

Industry View In-Line
We keep our 3Q26 iPhone build estimate unchanged at 54M units (+4% q/q, -2%
y/y). But we have made adjustments to new iPhone 18 builds by lowering iPhone
Duo by 0.5M units to 0.5M, while raising iPhone 18 Pro Max by 0.5M units to 8.5M.

For 4Q, we lower our iPhone build estimate by 1M units to 72M units (+33% q/q,
-5% y/y), reflecting lower legacy iPhone models (iPhone 16/17e). Total new iPhone
18 model family units remains at 71M units (-21% y/y) in 2H26, with one less SKU
launched compared to last year. We continue to expect 7M units of iPhone Duo
builds in 2H26. With iPhone Duo sales starting soon, we will continue to monitor
ramp-up progress for any upside or downside to builds. Our revised full-year iPhone

build forecast implies CY26 shipment at 234M units (+3% y/y).
Our 3Q26 iPad build forecast is lowered by 0.5M units to 11.5M units (-12% q/q, -
18% y/y). We lowered 3Q entry level iPad builds due to  pull-forward demand in 1H
and the impact of rising prices. We also lower our 4Q iPad build forecast by 0.7M
units to 10.3M units (-10% q/q, -26% y/y). Our revised full-year iPad build forecast
implies CY26 shipments at 46.8M units (-14% y/y).

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

Rather than now slowing down, it’s actually getting stronger
TSMC reported an upbeat 3Q26 result with robust 4Q26 outlook. It guided for
4Q26 revenue of US$52.3–54.3 billion, representing about 14% QoQ growth and
58% year-over-year growth at the midpoint (based on FX at NT$31.8 per USD).
4Q26 GM/OPM is expected at 65–67% and 56.5–58.5%. TSMC reiterated its 2026
capex to be US$60–64 billion and said investment from 2026 to 2028 will be

significantly higher than in the previous three years, reflecting strong multi-year
customer demand, including agentic AI. It is also worth noting that TSMC plans to
begin using the High-NA EUV technology around 2030 and develop a 12-inch
mask capability by 2031, when it expects the tools to offer sufficient maturity and
cost effectiveness. Management said this timing does not undermine its
confidence in technology leadership.
Management described AI-related demand as exceptionally strong, supported by

accelerators, networking chips, and rising demand for CPUs associated with
agentic AI. TSMC’s previously guided AI GPU/accelerator CAGR to be mid-to-high
50%, but now sees more upside ahead. Given rising concern on AI expanding
peaking out, the management said it is clear that its capacity planning strategy has
considered customer forecasts, data-center construction, power availability, and
other supply-chain constraints. Management still judges demand to be above

available supply and is working to close the gap, while emphasizing disciplined
planning to limit the risk of overbuilding.
Figure 1. TSMC – 3Q26 results comparison
© 2026 Citigroup Inc. No redistribution without Citigroup’s written permission.
Source: Citi Research, Bloomberg
Capacity expansion plan and allocation
During the earnings call, TSMC also mentioned that despite the tight capacity
supply, customers cannot generally sublet or auction wafer capacity, while there is

limited case that collaboration is through mask-license agreements. For the
advanced packaging, TSMC has announced its Global Foundries and VIS’
agreement on interposer as part of its backend supply strategy, intended to help
ensure customers have enough capacity to bring products to market; It also
reiterated that TSMC’s CoWoS remains as the leading customer’s choice and is
already in volume production at 5x reticle size, while it is developing 9x and larger

solutions and evaluating CoPoS.

TSMC (2330.TW)
15 October 2026 Citi Research
4
Reiterate Buy, best position in AI arena
Our earnings projection is largely unchanged for 2027 /2028 earnings. While its
capex expansion, overseas operation and N2 will likely be a margin dilution factor
initially, due to its cross-node leverage, high UTR and yield rate, we believe its GM
downside is manageable. Overall, we see strong advanced node demand into next
year and the long-term order visibility will be supported for higher capex cycle.  We

maintain our TP to NT$4,000, based on unchanged 25x of 2027 EPS estimate;
reiterate Buy.

n4Q26 guidance
–Sales: US$52.3-54.3bn (+14% QoQ at the midpoint)
–GPM: 65-67% (assuming USDTWD=31.8)
–OPM: 56.5-58.5%
n2026 full-year & long-term guidance
–Full year sales: raise 2026 full-year USD-based sales guidance from more
than 40% growth to +44% growth
–Full year capex: retain its 2026 capex guidance at US$60-64bn (70-80%
advanced process, 10% specialty, 10-20% for advanced packaging, testing

and masks)
–GM outlook
•4Q GM to be impacted by N2 and overseas fab ramps, but partially
supported by productivity improvement, UTR enhancement and
optimizing capacity allocation
•GM dilution from overseas fab would be c2ppts impact for the full-year
2026; with more new capacity expansion in the US, the margin dilution
impact would widen to 3-4ppts in the next few years.
–Long-term outlook
•Retains 5-year AI sales CAGR (2024-2029) remains at high-50% or above

nCapacity planning
–TSMC currently has 20 fabs in total. It plans to add 13 fabs in Taiwan and add
5-6 overseas fabs.
–N2 capacity will double in 2027; it has also started preparing for A14 capacity
with volume production starting from 2028.
–US Arizona fab 2 construction is complete and begin volume production in
3Q27, ahead of schedule.
–JP fab 2 would produce N3 with mass production from 2028

Bull/Bear: TSMC (2330.TW)

### Page 10

TSMC (2330.TW)
15 October 2026 Citi Research
10
TSMC
Company description
TSMC is the founder and leader of the dedicated IC foundry segment. The
company has built its reputation by offering advanced and "more-than-
Moore" wafer production processes and industry-leading manufacturing
efficiency. From its inception in 1987, TSMC has consistently offered the
foundry segment's leading technologies and TSMC-compatible design
services.
Investment strategy

We rate TSMC shares as Buy. We expect TSMC to deliver sales growth of
>40% in recent years thanks to strong growth from HPC/AI as well as stable
demand growth from smartphone, Io T, and automotive on tech migration and
a shortened replacment cycle. CPU outsourcing to TSMC offers further
growth potential. A rising cash dividend should also support the share price.
While TSMC has been increasing its capex meaningfully since 2009, its cost
structure remaisn competitive through continuous product mix

improvements, efficiency enhancements, and cost reductions.
Valuation
Our target price for TSMC shares of NT$4,000 is based on 25x our 2027E
EPS, higher than its three-year average forward PER, versus global peers’
average of 16-25x. We believe our target PER is justified by TSMC's leading
position in advanced process node and robust AI demand growth outlook.
Given its continuing leadership in the semiconductor foundry industry, we
believe TSMC’s stable order visibility and earnings outlook make it less

vulnerable to a potential global economic downturn than some of its peers.
Our target price equates to 2026E/27E P/E of ~ 36x/25x and 2026E/27E P/B of
~13x/10x.
Risks
Key downside risks that could impede the shares from achieving our target
price include: 1) weakness in the global semiconductor market; 2) larger-
than-expected margin contractions due to depreciation cost increases and
strong NTD; 3) competitors entering the foundry business; 4) longer-than-

expected digestion of inventory in the supply chain; and 5) a slowdown in
demand either due to a global economic downturn or a global trade
disruption triggered by tariffs.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

our ratings distribution table for our Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside rating system. However, we reiterate that we do
not consider No View to be a recommendation. For all Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside calls, risk exists that the
catalyst(s) and associated share-price movement will not materialize as expected.

### Page 1

삼성바이오로직스 (207940/KS) 
3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 
2026-10-06

제약/바이오. 이선경 / seonkyoung.lee@sks.co.kr / 3773-9089

매수(유지) 
목표주가: 2,000,000 원(유지) 
현재주가: 1,354,000 원 
상승여력: 47.7%

Signal: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 전망  
Key: 5 공장 매출 본격화, 미국 공장 매출 기여에 따른 성장 기대   
Step: 단기 불확실성보다는 중장기적 성장 방향성에 주목

3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 성장지속, 컨센 부합 전망   
26년 3분기 연결기준 매출액은 1조 3,741억원(+9.3%YoY, +4.0%QoQ), 영업이
익은 6,098억원(-3.7%YoY, +4.0%QoQ, OPM 44.4%)으로 컨센서스 매출액 1
조 3,450억원과 영업이익 6,063억원에 부합하는 실적을 전망한다.  
5월 파업 영향으로 약 20개 배치 생산분(약 1,500억원)에 대한 원가 손실이 인식되
고 3분기 평균 환율이 전분기 대비 약 5% 하락했음에도 불구하고, 3분기부터 매출로 
인식되는 5공장 PPQ 배치 및 미국 공장의 매출 기여로 지속적인 성장이 예상된다. 약 
1,500억원 규모의 생산 차질분은 고객사와 일정 조율을 통해 출하될 예정으로 이에 
따른 4분기 매출 성장을 기대해 볼 수 있다.   
신규 수주 회복과 모달리티 확장에 주목 
시장에서 우려했던 신규 수주는 관세 이슈로 고객사의 컨택이 감소하면서 올해 저조했
으나 4 월 Section 232 결과 발표 이후 논의가 회복 단계에 있어 연내 추가 신규 수주 
가능성은 열려 있다.  
항체 시장의 경쟁심화와 차세대 모달리티 확장에 대응하기 위해 결정한 폴리펩타이드
의 인수는 연내 마무리될 예정이다. 폴리펩타이드의 2028 년 목표 EBITDA 마진은 
25%로 동사 대비 낮은 상황이나, 펩타이드 기반 CDMO 시장 성장에 따른 마진 개선
도 가능하다. 현재 임단협, 환율, 미국 공장 및 폴리펩타이드 인수에 따른 회계 영향 등 
불확실성이 큰 상황이나, 수주 회복과 5 공장 매출 인식 본격화, 미국 공장 및 멀티 모
달리티 확장 등 중장기 성장 방향성에 주목할 필요가 있다.

본 보고서에 언급된 종목의 경우 당사 조사분석담당자는 본인의 담당종목을 보유하고 있지 않습니다.

당사는 자료공표일 현재 해당기업과 관련하여 특별한 이해 관계가 없습니다.

## Document 196: [207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대단기 부담_2a8cdc97

Source PDF: all report pdf/[207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대단기 부담_2a8cdc97.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

(26.10.02) 
1,354,000 
제약업종

◼ 3Q26 컨센서스 부합, 4Q26 성장폭 확대 전망 
◼ 5공장 Ramp-up 지속, 신규 수주 회복 여부에 주목 
◼ 투자의견 매수, 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지

투자의견 매수, 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지 
목표주가는 DCF 방식으로 산출(WACC 8.1%, 영구성장률 3.5% 적용)

3Q26 Preview: 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 전망 
3Q26 매출액 1조 3,380억원(+6.4% yoy, +1.3% qoq), 영업이익 5,890억원(-
7.0% yoy, +0.4% qoq, OPM 44.0%)으로 컨센서스에 부합하는 실적을 전
망함. 3분기 평균 원/달러 환율이 전분기 대비 약 5% 하락하고, 지난 5월 
파업에 따른 생산 차질 영향이 반영되나, 5공장 PPQ 물량의 매출 인식이 
시작되며 yoy, qoq 외형 성장은 이어질 것으로 예상함.  
파업 당시 생산이 지연된 물량은 3Q 중 추가 생산되어 4Q26부터 매출에 반
영될 전망임. 5공장은 다수 프로젝트의 PPQ가 진행 중이며, Ramp-up에 
따라 매출 기여도 점진적으로 확대될 것으로 예상함. 이에 4Q26에는 파
업 이연 물량 반영과 5공장 매출 확대에 따라 실적 성장폭이 확대될 전
망임. 2026년 연간 매출액과 영업이익은 각각 5조 5,310억원(+21.4% 
yoy), 2조 4,780억원(+19.8% yoy, OPM 44.8%)으로 전망함.

신규 수주 회복 기대, 중장기 성장 기반은 확대 중 
관세 관련 불확실성으로 둔화됐던 고객사와의 수주 논의가 재개되고 있음. 
10월 2일 유럽 소재 제약사와 935억원 규모의 CMO 계약을 추가 체결
했으며, 4Q26 신규 수주 회복 여부에 주목함.  
PolyPeptide 인수는 연내 거래 종결을 목표로 진행 중이며, 인수 완료 
시 펩타이드 분야로 CDMO 포트폴리오가 확대될 전망임. 6 공장도 연내 
착공을 목표로 준비 중임. 
한편, 노사 리스크는 단기 불확실성 요인임. 노조는 10/26부터 3주간 2
차 총파업을 예고함. 1차 대비 예정된 파업 기간이 길어진 만큼 실제 파
업 진행 시 생산 차질 규모가 주요 변수임. 다만 파업 개시 전까지 협상 
가능성이 열려 있어, 향후 노사 협상 진행 경과를 확인할 필요가 있음.

## Document 198: [207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 불확실성보다 성장을 볼 시점삼성바이오로직스(207940) 불확실성보다 성장을 볼 시점불확실성보다 성장_3801b631

Source PDF: all report pdf/[207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 불확실성보다 성장을 볼 시점삼성바이오로직스(207940) 불확실성보다 성장을 볼 시점불확실성보다 성장_3801b631.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
3분기 실적 컨센서스 상회 
삼성바이오로직스의 2026년 3분기 매출액은 1조 4,903억 원(+18.5% Y oY), 
영업이익은 6,401억 원(+1.0% Y oY, 영업이익률 43.0%)으로 시장 컨센서스를 각각 
10%, 5% 상회했다. 1~4공장의 풀가동이 이어진 가운데 5공장의 인증용 생산배치 
물량과 미국 락빌 공장 매출(약 1,000억 원)이 처음으로 반영되며 외형 성장을 
견인했다. 매출원가율은 50.4%로 전년 동기(41.4%), 전분기(47.0%) 대비 
상승했는데, 락빌 공장과 5공장의 초기 물량 반영에 따른 것으로 판단한다. 향후 
물량 상승에 따라 매출원가율은 감소할 것으로 예상한다. 그럼에도 EBITDA 
마진율은 50.4%로 높은 수준을 유지하며 7,515억 원을 달성했다. 순이익은 원/달러 
환율 하락에 따른 외화환산손실이 반영되며 3,911억 원을 기록했다.  
환율 하락에도 가이던스 상향하며 성장 궤도 유지 
2026년 연간 매출액은 5조 6,133억 원(+23.2% Y oY), 영업이익은 2조 4,605억 
원(+18.9% YoY)으로 전망한다. 최근 원/달러 환율 하락에도 이번 실적발표에서 
상향 제시한 연간 매출 성장률 20% 이상은 충분히 달성 가능할 것으로 판단한다. 
4분기에는 락빌 공장의 매출 약 1,000억 원과 5공장 가동 확대에 더해, 5월 
파업으로 이연된 물량의 매출 인식이 성장에 기여할 전망이다.  
투자의견 매수 및 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지 
비만 치료제 CDMO 진출을 위한 폴리펩타이드 그룹 인수는 공개매수 응모율 
87.88%로 최소 조건을 충족해 11월 말 완료를 앞두고 있어 신규 성장 동력은 
확보된 것으로 판단한다. 노사 리스크도 법원의 쟁의행위 제한 확대에 이어 노조가 
2차 총파업을 잠정 연기하며 크게 완화됐다. 미국 관세 불확실성으로 부진했던 신규 
수주 모멘텀은 올해 연말부터 내년에 걸쳐 강화될 것으로 전망하며, 불확실성 
해소와 성장 기대감을 반영할 시점이라 판단한다. 
목표주가 (유지) 2,000,000원

## Document 249: Capital Market Outlook (2026-10-05)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Capital Market Outlook (2026-10-05).pdf
Report source: Merrill Lynch (CMO)
Date: 2026-10-05
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Stronger Growth, Costlier Money
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
The latest GDP report and annual revisions show a sturdier U.S. economy than previously
reported. According to the Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA), real GDP grew at a 2.2%
annualized pace in Q2 compared to 1.5% previously estimated, and Q1 was revised
from 2.1% to 2.5%. Both 2024 and 2025 were also revised slightly higher. Most of the

first half of the year revision was in categories related to the Artificial Intelligence (AI)
buildout, but real consumer spending has also been revised slightly higher, accelerating
from 0.7% annualized growth in Q1 to 3.8% in Q2 despite surging energy prices and
slow hiring.
Spending in July and August remained much stronger than labor market conditions alone
would suggest, also averaging 3.7% annualized growth. Upside revisions to net interest

income and personal saving suggest households have benefited from more income than
was previously apparent, with personal saving revised higher back to 2023. The new data,
according to the BEA, show the January to July 2026 savings rate averaged 4.8% versus
3.3% prerevision, strengthening consumer spending underpinnings despite moderate
hiring, high energy prices and rising borrowing costs.
The BEA update also included inflation methodological changes related to categories

inflated by the AI boom—such as portfolio management fees and computer software and
accessories. These improved the inflation picture to some extent—August “core” PCE
inflation was reduced by 0.3 ppts to 3% YoY, bringing it closer to “core” consumer price
index inflation, which was 2.5% YoY and 2% on a three-month annualized basis. While still
above the 2% Federal Reserve (Fed) target, “core” PCE now appears stable at 3% year over
year since January rather than accelerating. These methodological changes are expected

to dampen pass-through of AI-related price pressures into “core” inflation and effects of
stock-market swings on inflation.
They also produced an encouraging improvement in recent PCE inflation momentum.
Three-month annualized “core” PCE inflation slowed from 4.3% at the peak of tariff
effects in February to 2.1% in August. For durable goods, this rate was down from 5.8%
in February to 1.5% in August, and, for services ex-energy and healthcare, it slowed from

4.2% to 2.2%, for example. Inflation is volatile on a three-month change basis, so it
remains to be seen whether this moderation continues without further Fed tightening.
For now, the trends in the San Francisco Fed’s cyclical versus acyclical inflation measures
diverged again in August. As expected, given softening wage growth, prices most sensitive
to the labor market have eased further. At 3% YoY, this inflation category is down from
8% in early 2023 and 4.6% two years ago is back to prepandemic levels and is likely

to ease more (Exhibit 1A). In contrast, supply bottlenecks, high energy prices and other
industry-specific factors have kept acyclical inflation on an uptrend. As shown in Exhibit
1B, the relentless increase in freight costs could prolong upside pressure on this category.
The diesel market has tightened in recent months, boosting its price by 70% YoY,
consistent with Brent oil at around $200 per barrel and large reverberations across
a wide swath of goods and services prices. A government-policy-related decline in

available truck drivers is also raising transportation costs more than expected. So are
tight ocean shipping capacity and longer/more expensive voyages due to the U.S./Iran
War. Supply-chain stress also increased as companies rushed to front-run potential new
tariff increases.
On the positive side for the inflation outlook, unit labor costs (ULC) are up modestly
as robust productivity growth has absorbed much of the wage growth, limiting a major
source of domestic inflation. Upside productivity growth revisions are likely to further

reduce ULC, which could help shift focus from energy and AI-related inflation to
eventual disinflationary effects once oil and refined fuel prices start to ease. Sustained
productivity gains would support growth and margins while limiting inflation and the
need for much higher interest rates.
Investment Implications
We favor selective Equity exposure
to companies with durable demand
and pricing power, while remaining
cautious about businesses most

exposed to fuel, freight, and
financing costs. We would also
gradually increase long-duration
exposure.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Surging nominal growth has boosted corporate revenues (up 13% YoY in Q2, according to the
BEA) while contained unit labor costs helped raise margins and profits more than expected.
With labor costs declining as a share of revenues, investment-related tax incentives and
rock-bottom net interest expense, pre-tax nonfinancial corporate profits as measured in GDP
accounts rose 21% YoY in Q2. Total corporate profits after-tax were up 18.2% YoY, according to

the BEA.
Surging AI-related imports and investment are keeping the business and profits cycle on
an uptrend both here and abroad. According to BofA Global Research, the global economic
cycle backdrop improved further in September, as reflected in a broad-based increase in its
Global Wave1 indicator. Broadening U.S. manufacturing strength, fueled by ongoing AI-driven
investment, a healthier orders backlog and low inventories suggest that the expansion has

room to run—a constructive environment for earnings. Typically, a Global Wave upswing is
consistent with rising earnings revisions ratios and equity price uptrends. According to the
Global Wave, global earnings growth expectations remain exceptionally strong.
Still, absent a resolution of war-related energy and transportation disruptions, the same
industrial strength intensifies inflation pressures. A prolonged supply shock raises the risk of
restrictive interest rates and weakening economic growth. In this context, the surge in the

10-year Treasury yield above 5% is starting to reflect more than optimism about U.S. growth
and strengthening productivity outlook. Oil’s tightest recent correlation with 10-year Treasury
yields in 35 years suggests a common energy-price thread behind rising yields in developed
countries.
Bond market spillovers from normalizing Japan yields and capital repatriation are another
common thread. Higher yields on foreign sovereign bonds such as in Japan—which has

escaped stagnation and deflation by intensely pursuing investment-led growth—weaken the
relative appeal of Treasurys. That distinction matters for portfolios. A yield increase driven
mainly by durable productivity gains would come with a stronger outlook for future income
and earnings. A yield increase driven by cost shocks and greater uncertainty raises financing
costs without the same benefit.
In sum, the economy remains more resilient than expected, but the bond market is also

charging more for an uncertain inflation outlook given untethered government debt and
cooling global demand for Treasurys spurred by geopolitical tensions and Japan capital
repatriation. The case for much further tightening becomes stronger if wage growth
strengthens, productivity lags, and unit labor costs reaccelerate, causing cyclical inflation to
also pick up steam. The case becomes weaker if that measure stays contained while fuel and
freight pressures recede or rising Treasury yields reduce asset prices and economic growth.

Exhibit 1: Softer Cyclical/Higher Acyclical Inflation Keeps “Core” PCE at 3% Year Through August.
A) Cooling wage pressures ease cyclical inflation (housing, food services and accommodation,
etc.).
B) Surge in U.S. AI-related imports, tariff front-running, shipping disruptions and fuel- price
spike feed acyclical inflation.

### Page 4

4 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Yields, Yen and Iran: What Haunts the “House”
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
We recently highlighted the three-front challenge facing Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent:
engineering a stronger yen, containing the 10-year Treasury yield and securing a diplomatic
resolution to the conflict with Iran.

Bessent recently has delivered one of the year’s more memorable lines while discussing
Treasury policy and the yen: “I have asymmetric information. I am the house now.” 2 Yet several
challenges remain: the 10-year Treasury yield is near levels last seen in the early 2000s, the
yen remains structurally weaker, and a phased agreement to reopen the Strait of Hormuz
appears remote. The resulting strain on energy markets keeps inflation risks elevated.

Put differently, oil prices, Treasury yields and the dollar-yen exchange rate are all trending
against Bessent’s objectives. Below, we examine how each variable is haunting his “house.”
Haunting the “House” is the Yen. Since the U.S. and Japan mounted a record $98.7B
coordinated intervention in late July after the yen weakened to a 40-year low near 164 per
dollar, the currency has appreciated roughly 4% against the dollar. The move was further
supported on September 18 when the BoJ raised rates by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%,

their highest level since the mid-1990s (Exhibit 2A). However, the split 7–2 vote and Governor
Ueda’s limited forward guidance leave the path of further tightening uncertain. The key
question is whether additional BoJ hikes can produce sustained appreciation in the yen, or
merely reinforce the temporary relief provided by the summer intervention.
Bessent, meanwhile, has repeatedly signaled his preference for a stronger yen. Partly because
yen stability is also a U.S. financing concern. Persistent currency weakness could prompt

Japan to support its currency by selling foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys—which would
be an unwelcome prospect as Washington partially relies on foreign capital flows to fund its
large fiscal deficits. That potential “haunts” the Treasury Secretary.  At precisely the moment
Washington needs foreign capital to finance its enormous fiscal deficits, it would be inopportune
for one of America’s largest creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities.

Haunting the “House” are Yields. The surge in yields runs counter to a White House intent
on lowering borrowing costs. The benchmark 10-year yield has broken above the 5% threshold
and climbed to levels not seen since 2007. At around 5.30%, it stands more than a full
percentage point above where it began the year (Exhibit 2B). The level is striking but so is the
speed of the move: Stronger-than-expected economic data, including a survey showing U.S.

2 September 8, 2026.

complicating the macro backdrop

### Page 5

5 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
business activity expanded at the fastest pace in more than five years, helped trigger an 18
bps surge in the 10-year yield—among its largest daily moves in decades. The 5-year yield also
topped 5% for the first time since 2007, while the 30-year yield reached 5.63%, its highest
level since 2002.
Compounding the move is inflation, running above the Fed’s 2% target for 67 months,

large fiscal deficits and the prospect of continued growth in Treasury issuance. Against this
backdrop, Treasury bought back $4.1 billion of long-dated debt, versus a maximum of $6
billion. That effort has done little to arrest the selloff. A subsequent auction priced nearly $4
billion of 7-year notes at 5.085%, the highest yield for that maturity in 33 years.
All in, the MOVE index, a key gauge of Treasury market volatility, jumped from 78 to 95 on

Wednesday September 23 and then to 104 the following day, its highest level since the early
days of the Iran war (Exhibit 3A).
Haunting the “House” is the Iran conflict. Early expectations that military pressure and
economic sanctions would quickly bring Iran to the negotiating table have yet to materialize.
Iranian officials have signaled that a full reopening of the Strait of Hormuz remains unlikely
while sanctions stay in place. Although talks between Washington and Tehran are continuing,

conflicting signals and lingering skepticism from both sides have kept tensions elevated.
As a result, oil prices have remained elevated in a range even as some Middle East supply
disruptions began to ease.
Startling is that Brent crude is up more than 60% this year, but refined product prices
have risen even faster. Ongoing disruptions to energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz,
combined with Ukrainian strikes on Russian refining infrastructure have tightened global

supplies of crude oil and refined fuels. Saudi Arabia has restored some drone-disrupted flows,
providing roughly 3.5 million barrels a day of relief (roughly 3% of global consumption).
The squeeze is mostly acute in diesel markets, where inventories remain constrained and
prices continue to climb (Exhibit 3B). U.S. diesel prices recently surpassed $6.50 per gallon,
the highest level on record, just as Russia has extended restrictions on diesel exports into

October. Against this backdrop, proposals to ban U.S. fuel exports risks worsening the
imbalance. According to S&P Global, an export ban could reduce refinery runs by nearly 2
million barrels per day and significantly cut gasoline production, potentially driving gasoline
and jet fuel prices even higher. As long as tensions persist, the oil impulse remains a key
impediment to structurally lower inflation.
Investment implications. Treasury Secretary Bessent increasingly sits at the intersection of

monetary policy, fiscal policy, foreign policy and national security. We remain constructive on
the U.S. economy and overweight U.S. Equities but are watching as these fronts challenge the
narrative. There is a great deal riding on the “house” versus the key macro variables of today.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
When It Comes to Attracting Foreign Capital, the U.S. Remains Exceptional
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
There may be no better vote of confidence in a country than the willingness of
foreigners to put their money there. And by that measurement, the U.S. remains the
exception—both portfolio and FDI inflows are currently running at record highs based

on the latest data. This dynamic is one key reason why we remain constructive on U.S.
assets over the longer term.
Notwithstanding all the handwringing about America’s fiscal deficits, tariffs and rising
government debt, global investors sunk some $942B in net purchases of U.S. Equities in
the 12 months to July, the highest rolling 12-month total on record. In Q2, net purchase
of U.S. Equities soared to $426B, up 62% from the same period a year ago (Exhibit 4A).

By country, flows were down from China—not surprisingly—but strong from Taiwan and
South Korea, who have taken to recycling their massive AI-generated trade surplus back
into the U.S. In the end, strong portfolio inflows suggest that international investors
remain willing to increase their exposure to the world's largest capital market—that’s
bullish for U.S. assets.
Meanwhile, Exhibit 4B highlights the growing push among foreign multinationals to be

inside the U.S., with FDI inflows totaling nearly $240B in the first six months of this year.
That is a staggering 103% jump from a year ago and means FDI inflows are on track for
a record year, estimated at $480B for 2026.
There are multiple factors behind surging inflows: One, due to rising U.S. trade
protectionism, foreign multinationals increasingly want to be “inside” the U.S. economy,
not outside. Two, within the $32 trillion-plus economy with extraordinarily consumer

purchasing power, foreign firms prefer to be closer to their customers via investment
than just trade (exports). Three, America’s massive investment cycle in AI, data centers,
semiconductors, power generation, etc., has created numerous potential investment
opportunities for many foreign firms that don’t want to miss out on America’s surging
capex cycle. Four, and finally, abundant energy supplies, deep capital markets and a highly
developed technological ecosystem have also induced more FDI inflows.

In the end, double-barreled capital inflows speak to the absolute and relative
attractiveness of the U.S. economy and capital markets. Stay invested in America—the
rest of the world is.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

ICE BofA MOVE Index is a market-implied measure of U.S. bond market volatility.
Consumer Price Index measures the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a market basket of consumer goods and services.
Baltic Dry Index is a daily shipping and trade index published by the Baltic Exchange in London that measures the cost of moving raw materials like iron ore, coal, and grain by sea.
Volatility Index (VIX) is a real-time market indicator that measures the stock market's expected volatility over the next 30 days.

or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.

investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: mulling the minutes
By Sian Fenner, Ryan Wells & Illiana Jain
11:10 October 09 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
The only data release of note in Australia this week was the October Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment
Survey, which reported a 4.7% drop in the headline index. At 80.4, sentiment has tumbled back to
deeply pessimistic levels, similar to the post-pandemic cost-of-living shock when readings were among

the weakest in the survey’s more than fifty-year history. Indeed, inflation and interest rate pressures are
once again clearly front of mind for consumers, with both the view on ‘family finances vs a year ago’

### Page 2

and ‘family finance next 12 months’ falling 8.0% and 6.4% in the month, respectively. Moreover, the
RBA’s September rate hike looks to have had a significant impact – sentiment tanked 20% between
those surveyed before the RBA decision and those surveyed afterwards.

Views on the economic outlook were relatively more resilient, but they still weakened, with the one-year
and five-year ahead sub-indexes falling 1.6% and 0.4% respectively. Consumers are becoming more
‘on edge’ about job security, albeit not outright alarmed. The unemployment rate remains relatively low
versus history, although it has drifted up to a cycle high of 4.6% as labour supply continues to outstrip
demand. We expect it to hold there in next week’s September Labour Force Survey – see our preview
for more detail. All told, with higher interest rates, renewed inflation pressures and a softening jobs
market all weighing at once, households are entering the final months of the year on the back foot.

Offshore, the focus was on US data.

Before turning to this week's releases, it is worth noting that September non-farm payrolls came in
softer than expected across most metrics. Employment rose by only 29k (89k expected), and there
were sizeable downward revisions to prior months, with a combined 60k removed from July and August
payrolls, leaving gains of just 31k and 29k respectively. The details pointed to weaker hiring in leisure &
hospitality and education & health services industries, although construction and manufacturing
employment continued to benefit from ongoing data-centre-related investment. The unemployment rate
edged up to 4.2%, with softer employment occurring alongside a rise in labour force participation to
61.8%. Meanwhile wage growth moderated to 0.1%mth (3.0%yr). Taken together, the report suggests
labour demand is cooling, with the three-month average pace of job creation slowing to around 50k.
However, softer payroll gains are not necessarily indicative of labour market deterioration. Lower
immigration and ageing demographics have reduced the pace of employment growth needed to keep
the unemployment rate stable. As such, the report is more consistent with a labour market that is
gradually normalising than one entering a meaningful downturn.

The September FOMC minutes reinforced a distinctly hawkish message, with participants noting they
had "not seen sufficient progress on lowering inflation in recent months" and expressing concern that
inflation risks remained "skewed to the upside". Policymakers highlighted that both core services and
core goods inflation remained elevated, with some attributing persistent goods price strength to the
effects of the AI buildout despite the sector's traditional disinflationary role. There was also concern
that, after more than five years of inflation above target, elevated inflation could begin to influence
inflation expectations and wage- and price-setting behaviour. In contrast, labour market conditions
were generally viewed as stable and "close to maximum employment". While a majority noted the
labour market had strengthened somewhat recently, several participants highlighted unusually low
dynamism, characterised by low hiring, low layoffs, a low job-finding rate and elevated long-term
unemployment. Importantly, participants generally judged that aggregate wage growth was moderate
and "consistent with inflation moving toward 2 percent", with several explicitly noting that "the labor
market was not currently a source of inflationary pressures".

### Page 3

Related articles
A key focus was the interaction between AI investment, productive capacity and inflation. While
participants broadly expected AI-related investment to boost productivity growth and potential output
over time, several warned that "the AI buildout could cause aggregate demand to outpace aggregate
supply over the medium term, putting upward pressure on inflation". Consistent with this view, some
participants had raised their estimate of the neutral policy rate and several judged the current policy
stance to be "not restrictive or only mildly restrictive". Against this backdrop, all participants supported
the September rate increase, and most assessed that another increase in the federal funds rate would
likely be appropriate by year-end, although future decisions would remain data dependent.

The September ISM Services PMI released this week painted a similar picture of an economy that
remains resilient but is gradually losing momentum. The headline index eased to 54.9 from 55.4 in
August, with the decline driven by a sharp fall in business activity and a 9.4pt drop in new export
orders, which slipped into contractionary territory for the first time in eight months. Labour market
conditions improved modestly with the employment index moving to broadly neutral territory 50.1 after
two months below 50, supported by elevated backlogs and still-solid demand. Respondent
commentary illustrated the mixed nature of labour demand, with some firms reporting they were "filling
positions vacated due to promotions or retirements", while others cited "restructuring due to efficiencies
gained using AI tools". Cost pressures also remained elevated, with the prices paid index rising to 74.0,
its highest level since July 2022 and around 18pts above its historical average, while fuel costs, tariffs
and supply chain constraints remained the most frequently cited concerns. Overall, the report suggests
services activity remains consistent with ongoing expansion, albeit at a slower pace, while labour
demand and pricing pressures remain firm.

Overall, the data supports our view that the FOMC will move to a broadly neutral policy stance by the
end of the year with one more rate hike, although the case for a back-to-back move in October has
become less clear cut.

COUNTRY RISK PROFILE
: STRONG ECONOMIC GROWTH AND LOW PUBLIC 
AND

◼ 
 Strong factors support

and economic prospects
 :

a

s
olid comparative advantages that continue to attract large FDI inflows

 ;

g

 ;

 s
trong medium
 -
 term economic growth prospects, assuming

 -

 he excessive domestic debt level of the economy (excluding the government) and fragilities in the banking sector 
 – 
this is a bad

 ;

 low forex reserves
 , which

 , 
 despite current account surpluses and notably due to

provide good  
protect
ion 
against shocks, while

 is vulnerable to external shocks
 ;

 v
ery high exposure to physical climate risk.  
 C

 -

 are

 -
 level rise
 s 
and flooding
 .

◼ 
 Non transfer risk and currency risk
 : 
 V
ietnam  
 has enjoyed very 
 favourable

 until 2025
 , 
thanks to 
 large FDI inflows
 , 
recurrent

 -
 account surpluses  
and declining f
 oreign debt (
 which is moderate, at 
 less than  
30% of 
both GDP and exports). 
 This trend 
 has been

 in

as

 are 
 decreasing
 . Meanwhile
 , 
there is a 
persist
 ent 
 risk of 
 increasing 
 resident capital outflows 
 fuelling  
 some 
 VND 
 depreciation pressure  
 (
w
hereas  
 the potential for foreign 
capital outflows is limited
 )
. 
As

reserve cushion is weak
 , it would

time 
i
f there is

 .

 (Vietnam has a crawling band FX regime)

◼ 
 Sovereign risk
 : 
the government debt burden 
 is moderate and 
 will remain 
 so in the short term, even if the fiscal deficit is projected to 
deteriorate
 . The fiscal deficit could exceed 3% of GDP in 2026 due to support measures in response to the energy shock (estimated at 
less than 1% of GDP in H1 2026), but this will not endanger public
 -
 debt dynamics. Government debt should fall again in 2027 thanks 
to lower deficits and fast GDP growth. Debt was only 30% of GDP in 2025 (vs. 41% in 2019) and interest payments consumed less  
than 5% of fiscal revenue (vs. 7.2% in 2019). The government cover
 s its funding needs mostly in the local bond market, which is 
deepening gradually and not 
 highly 
 exposed to foreign investors. Government debt in foreign currency declined to about 25% of total 
debt in 2025 (vs. 38% in 2019); this is still substantial, but almost all this FC debt 
 is made up  
 of concessional loans from official 
creditors. On the negative front, high credit risks in the state corporate and bank sectors 
 are 
 very large contingent risks for the 
sovereign. Moreover, public investment needs related to climate change and infrastructure development will increase in the 
 medium 
term
.

◼ 
 Systemic credit risk
 : 
Domestic credit to the private sector is too large and still rising (141% of GDP in 2025 vs. 108% in 2019) while 
the banking sector remains poorly capitali
 s
ed. Credit risks and resource misallocation have long been exacerbated by opacity and 
supervision flaws, as well as the use of high credit growth targets to support economic growth (+15% in 2026). In 2022
 –
2023, banks 
faced severe funding stress and asset
 -
 quality deterioration resulting from the property and bond market crisis. The situation has 
recovered gradually since then. In the 
 short term
 , credit risks may increase amid higher energy costs and if  
there is  
a correction in 
the tech cycle. Some banks, especially small private ones, also remain exposed to VND funding risk (whereas banks do not rely  
much 
on 
 foreign
-
 currency 
 financing). Positively, reforms, including stricter capital
 -
 adequacy requirements, continue to be introduced 
gradually 
 in order 
 to improve banks’ creditworthiness (
 the 
 SBV plans to test the removal of credit quotas for the soundest banks 
from 2026).

## Document 308: How bad is the rise in bond yields_ (2026-09-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/How bad is the rise in bond yields_ (2026-09-23).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-23
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

SPECIAL EDITION | HOW BAS IS THE RISE 
IN BOND YIELDS?

TRANSCRIPTION

0:00:10 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Everyone, welcome. As we head into autumn 2026, interest rates are reaching levels never seen for many, many years. 
Why is this happening? Is it sustainable? And what are the implications for the economies of different countries? All these 
questions will be discussed over the next few minutes with the economists of BNP Paribas. Let us begin with Isabelle 
Mateos y Lago, chief economist of the group. Hello, Isabelle.  
0:00:38 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everyone. 
0:00:39 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you for being with us today. Just to begin, a simple explanation. What rates are we talking about and which countries 
are affected? 
0:00:48 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, it is very important to clarify this. So, we are talking about long-term interest rates, rates at 10 years or longer 
maturities, 30 years, etc. The rates at which sovereign states borrow on the market. And this is primarily something that is 
happening in advanced countries, so United States, United King dom, Japan, and then the Eurozone countries. And indeed, 
as you said, we are seeing since the beginning of the summer, interest rates reach levels we had not seen for about 20 
years. But it is important to keep in mind that up until that point, up until the mid -2000s, the level of interest rates that 
we're seeing now were really quite common, quite normal, so one shouldn't overdramatize.  
0:01:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why do we find ourselves in this situation and how did we get to this point?  
0:01:41 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, so the direct cause is that investors are demanding higher interest rates in order to  lend their capital to governments. 
But why is this? Well, there's essentially three reasons for this, and not all of them are bad, and they're not mutually 
exclusive either. The top reason to my mind is that there's just greater competition for capital be tween sovereign states 
and the corporate sector, who are all facing simultaneously additional investment needs, in defense, in artificial 
intelligence, in energy transition, in resilience. And so,  you just have greater competition for capital and that pushes up the 
price of capital, which is the interest rate. 
Reason number two is because markets have revised upwards their expectations of how high central banks are going to be 
taking their interest rates. 
And this mechanically translates into higher rates at the long end. And then there's a third reason, which doesn't apply 
necessarily in every case, but which is essentially higher sovereign risk, a kind of a risk premium, in cases where there are  
concerns arising about the sustainability of these public debts.

### Page 2

2

0:02:59 - Emmanuel Laborde 
What are the implications for global economies? 
0:03:03 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
So that's exactly what we'll be discussing in the next segments. But basically, the two key issues are, one, an adverse 
impact on public finances. It's higher interest rates making it harder to balance the books in terms of public accounts. And 
then secondly, these higher rates tend to slow down economic growth. Of course, by how much and how serious it will be, 
depends on how high interest rates go and how long they stay there. But that's for the next segments.  
0:03:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Yes. Thank you very much, Isabelle. Having said the scene now, let's move on to the first segment with the following 
question. What makes debt sustainable? And let's see what Hélène Baudchon has to say.  
Hello Hélène and welcome into this special edition. 
0:03:58 - Hélène Baudchon 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everybody. 
0:04:01 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When economists talk about public debt, they often refer to the sustainability of debt. As an economist, can you explain to 
us what that means and what is the debt trajectory deemed unsustainable?  
0:04:15 - Hélène Baudchon 
To begin with, I will say that a temporary increase in debt can be seen as a normal evolution, not particularly concerning, 
for instance during an economic crisis or more recently during a health crisis. In such cases, it acts as a buffer. A problem  
arises when the debt level never comes back down. An unsustainable trajectory can be therefore defined, characterized by 
a continuous regular rise in the debt level, both in absolute terms and as a proportion of GDP, driven by high and recurring 
fiscal deficits. And sometimes interest payments can exacerbate the problem.  
0:05:00 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In very simple terms, you are talking about the snowball effect.  
0:05:03 - Hélène Baudchon 
Exactly explaining why consolidation measures are needed in order to prevent such an adverse situation from occurring 
and in order to prevent, in fact, a sudden loss of confidence from creditors.  
0:05:16 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Regarding governments, what should serve as a warning sign for them? And at what point could the situation become, let's 
say, problematic? 
0:05:27 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, simply speaking, when interest rates rise, not so long ago, up until 2022, Eurozone countries were borrowing at very 
low rates and in some instance, even negative rates. And although debt levels were already at high level at that time, the 
low rates helped manage debt servicing. Not much fiscal effort was needed to keep the public debt ratios in check.  
0:05:55 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Of course, we all remember this because it was a very unusual situation, but now things are very different.

### Page 3

3

0:06:01 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed, the situation today is clearly more challenging simply because bond yields are significantly higher and they 
are rising at the time of our discussion. This is for the part the result of the ECB's monetary tightening in 2022 -2023 aimed 
at addressing the inflation shock then. Then long-term interest rates have risen as a consequence and have aligned more 
closely with GDP growth rate. And in technical terms, this is what we refer to as a narrowing of the "r" - "g" spread. 
0:06:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why is this spread so important? Why do you, the economists, monitor it this way?  
0:06:48 - Hélène Baudchon 
Essentially, you have the interest rate "r", which determines the debt burden, while the growth rate "g" determines the 
trajectory of fiscal revenues. And if "r" is lower than "g", and this has been the case for many years, as you can see on the  
chart on the screen, it means that interest payments are increasing at a slower pace than the revenue component of the 
equation. 
0:07:14 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this provides some leeway in other budgetary areas, such as education, of course, defense, maybe pensions.  
0:07:22 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed. And this is what we refer to as the primary budget. However, if and when interest rates do go up while the 
economy isn't growing that much faster, then fiscal leeway diminishes.  
0:07:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Crystal clear. Now, just could you provide a few last words to wrap up the sustainability segment?  
0:07:43 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, to conclude, we are entering a period which demands greater fiscal discipline or, as some might say, less indiscipline 
in order to manage debt trajectories effectively, in particular in the countries which have high debt levels. And for instanc e, 
the Italian case is an interesting one because it is well known for its high debt level ratio. But in fact, it also has an 
advantageous position because it has no primary deficit. In fact, it even benefits from a large primary surplus.  
0:08:22 - Emmanuel Laborde 
As for the countries... For the other countries... I stop you because this will be a discussion with Guillaume Derrien during  
the next segment. Thank you very much, Hélène. 
0:08:30 - Hélène Baudchon 
Thank you, Emmanuel. 
0:08:36 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Guillaume. Welcome. 
0:08:37 - Guillaume Derrien 
Thank you. 
0:08:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Emmanuel, and hello to everyone. The flexibility of fiscal policy depends heavily on interest rates, which have 
increased considerably over the past few months. The question is, where do we stand today?

### Page 4

4

0:08:52 - Guillaume Derrien 
So, long-term rates have increased in all advanced economies, reaching levels not seen since 2008 in Europe and in the 
United States, and even since 1996 in Japan. Fortunately, the effective rate that states pay on their debt is lower than thos e 
headline figures suggest, simply because the average maturity of debt is relatively long. Precisely how long are we talking 
about? So, it's about 10 years in Japan, 13 years in the United Kingdom, and 8.5 years in France. That means that most of 
the debt was raised before rates started climbing. 
For example, in France, the effective rate just recently rose above the 2% mark, which is significantly lower than the 10 -
year bond yield, which exceeded 4% for new issuances.  
That said, in the United States, which has an average maturity of roughly six years, is experiencing a more rapid increase in  
its effective rate. 
0:09:54 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this rate increase is happening in the context of high debt -to-GDP ratios and with modest growth, correct? Yes, that's 
correct. 
0:10:05 - Guillaume Derrien 
Debt levels have reached unprecedented heights since the end of World War II. And at the same time, growth is lower, 
hovering around 2% on average in the United States, 1% in Europe and 0.5% in Japan. What does that mean for debt 
servicing costs? So, debt servicing cost is already relatively high, but it is expected to grow further as debt issue before the 
2022 rate hikes mature and are replaced with new instruments bearing higher interest rates. By 2030, interest costs could 
rise to levels last seen in 2000, specifically in France, around 4% of GDP. 
Are all the countries equally affected by these trends? Actually, no. Some countries are managing to reduce their debt 
level. 
Spain, Italy, even Japan have sufficiently improved their primary balance to achieve this, at least for the time being. And o f 
course, we must speak about Germany. Yes, Germany is implementing investment programs that result in a higher public 
deficit and debt-to-GDP ratio. But this ratio started from a relatively low base at around above 60% of GDP. That said, in 
this current context, German bonds have risen. And because German bonds are used as a benchmark for Europe, it  raises 
rates across all Eurozone countries. Is France the only country seeing its public debt go up?  
No, it's not, Emmanuel. This is happening in the United Kingdom and in the United States. These economies, like France, 
are running public deficits that are ever close or exceed 5% of GDP, which is too large for debt ratio to stabilize on its ow n. 
France and the United Kingdom are trying to gradually reduce their public deficit so as not to compromise growth. The 
United States is not going on that path yet, but market pressure could eventually force them to do so.  
0:12:08 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much for your input, Guillaume. Thank you, Emmanuel. Let's go to the next segment. It's about the impact 
of rising bond yields on financing and investments in the Eurozone.  
0:12:26 - Thomas Humblot 
Hello, Thomas. Welcome. Hello. Thank you for having me today.  
0:12:30 - Emmanuel Laborde 
We've just seen what's behind the rise in Eurozone bond yields. This rise is partly attributed to the sovereign spread. What 
impact will this have on the economy? 
0:12:42 - Thomas Humblot 
Actually, this widening of some government bond spreads is obviously not good news, but it tends paradoxically to have 
less impact than an increase in long-term interest rates that does not include the sovereign risk premium. for example, in 
France, for a very long time, it has been considered that the 10 -year OAT was the relevant benchmark for fixed loans. And 
at that time, the spread on French government bonds was relatively stable. But since 2024, that spread increased

### Page 5

5

significantly and that renders the sovereign yield a less relevant benchmark.  
And now the most relevant benchmark is the swap rate because it determines the cost of bank funding that backs new 
loans. 
0:13:56 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Since the end of 2024, we have seen a rise in long-term 10-year swap rates. What impact does this have on bank lending 
rates? 
0:14:08 - Thomas Humblot 
Fixed loans have obviously become more expensive, but to a lesser extent than if they were priced solely based on long -
term sovereign rates. And what about existing fixed-rate loans? 
There is no impact. An increase in rates for the entire maturity of a loan does not have any effect if it is a fixed -rate loan. 
Borrowers with outstanding loans are therefore protected. Obviously, new fixed loans are granted at higher rates. And this 
sharply contrasts with a floating-rates loans. 
Because with floating-rates loans, borrowers experience regular rate adjustments throughout the term of the loan. And in 
terms of bank financing? Strictly in terms of bank financing, an increase in short -term rates has an immediate and 
widespread restrictive impact on borrowers, while for long-term interest rates, the impact is more gradual, and it comes 
gradually as the outstanding amount of fixed-rate loans are progressively renewed. And the thing is that since the 
beginning of the war in Iran, we have seen a combination of the two.  
And in practical terms, the rate of new bank loans to non-financial corporations has increased about 25 basis points in the 
eurozone since the beginning of the year. 
0:16:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Just a few words about companies that borrow directly from the market.  
0:16:16 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, in that case, the impact is even more straightforward. The rate at which a company borrows from the market is the 
risk-free rate plus a premium. 
0:16:28 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Have risk premiums, known as corporate credit spreads, also increased?  
0:16:34 - Thomas Humblot 
Yes, they have increased temporarily at the beginning of the year but have returned to normal since. For the time being, the 
widening remains moderate for the highest-rated investment-grade issuers. 
However, some significant bond issuance in the euro debt market due to some major US technology companies has 
increased spreads for longer maturities. 
0:17:11 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In particular terms, how does this affect the investment decisions of the companies?  
0:17:19 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, investments that were previously worthwhile due to returns sufficient to cover the cost of capital may no longer do so 
and in turn that might lead to a decrease in demand for financing.  
0:17:40 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much Thomas. Now let us move on to the emerging economies. And it is with Cynthia Kalasopatan -Antoine.

### Page 6

6

Hello Cynthia, and welcome. 
0:17:54 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Hi, Emmanuel. Hello, everyone. 
0:17:57 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Long-term bond yields rose significantly in advanced economies over the summer. What can we now say about emerging 
markets? 
0:18:06 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Emerging bond markets held up well, which is quite unusual. The rise in long -term sovereign yields in emerging countries 
has been less pronounced than in advanced economies, including the United States and Germany. Now, there are two 
main exceptions, Turkey and Poland. Meanwhile, we did not witness any significant correction in emerging financial 
markets over the summer, and the main currencies appreciated slightly against the dollar or the euro.  
0:18:37 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Does this mean that public finances are more robust in emerging countries?  
0:18:43 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, it depends. You see, in cases like the Czech Republic and South Korea, public debt is moderate and macroeconomic 
fundamentals are solid. However, these countries are now less frequently considered as emerging and tend to be viewed 
as newly advanced economies. By contrast, Brazil and South Africa have weaker public finances and investors have 
already incorporated a higher sovereign risk into their assessments. And this is reflected in the bond yields. And this 
assessment of sovereign risk did not really change over the summer. 
0:19:26 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When comparing the main fiscal metrics, what are the trends? What do you see as an economist?  
0:19:33 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
On average, the fiscal metrics in emerging markets are not substantially worse than those in advanced economies. The 
average budget deficit levels are similar, ranging from 4% to 5% of GDP in 2025 -2026, according to the IMF. And as in 
advanced economies, the public debt is higher than its pre-Covid level. However, the average public debt to GDP ratio in 
emerging countries excluding China is close to 60%, whereas in advanced economies, it is above 100%.  
0:20:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Do you arrive at the same conclusion when comparing debt sustainability criteria?  
0:20:17 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, there are significant regional differences. In emerging Asia, the factors that determine debt sustainability are more 
often favorable than those in advanced economies. In Central Europe, the gap between the interest rate paid on debt and 
the GDP growth rate is also favorable. However, the primary deficits in some countries are high.  
0:20:45 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In a few words, a brief overview of the situation in Latin America.  
0:20:49 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, Brazil and Mexico are in a less favorable position. These countries would indeed need to generate a significant 
primary budget surplus each year in order to stabilize their debt in the medium term.

### Page 7

7

0:21:07 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Economists use the term original sin, which refers to the situation when governments resort to borrowing in foreign 
currency to address the shortfall in domestic financing. Does this phenomenon continue to distinguish emerging 
countries? 
0:21:24 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Yes, this is still the case. Emerging countries still have a significant share of their debt in foreign currency. This repres ents 
about 35 to 40% of their total debt on average. And in fact, now, this figure has decreased over the past decade, and this 
contributes to their resilience. However, governments in emerging markets have a less favorable position as regards their 
debt profile and their financing costs are also on average higher.  
0:22:02 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And finally, just a few words about a larger subject, China.  
0:22:07 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
China's public debt is significant and growing rapidly. That being said, it is denominated in domestic currency and issued in  
domestic markets. This debt is, however, sustainable in the medium term due to a very low effective interest rate and a 
high growth rate. 
0:22:29 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Cynthia, for being with us today.  
0:22:32 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Thank you. 
0:22:33 - Emmanuel Laborde 
It is now time to conclude, and we welcome back Group Chief Economist Isabelle Mateos y Lago.  
It is time for our conclusion with Isabelle. Once again, just a final question, please. Could or should, I do not know which 
word is the good one, central banks intervene to curb this rise in interest rates?  
0:22:58 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Absolutely not. The main contribution that central banks can bring to dealing with this situation is to restore price stabili ty, 
as is their mandate, as promptly as possible. And in the current circumstances, this means keeping relatively high policy 
rates, in some cases, rising them further. And the responsibility for keeping public finances on a sustainable footing rests 
squarely with governments and any attempt by these governments to instead take the easy way out by pressuring central 
banks to lower interest rates prematurely would be enormously counterproductive.  
0:23:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Isabelle. Thank you all for joining us today. Just a few words to remind you that additional economic 
research content can be found by scanning the QR code shown on your screen. Do not hesitate to use it with your 
smartphone. It is very easy. It was a pleasure to be with you today. Have a nice day and hope to see you so

Stopping for gas
• We estimate that New Zealand consumer
prices rose by 0.7% in the September quarter,
with a moderation in fuel costs balanced
against increases in council rates, building
costs and food prices.
• The annual inflation rate is expected to drop
back to 3.7% (down from 4.1% in the year
to  June).
• Measures of core inflation are expected to
remain firm, with some closely watched
measures expected to rise to the top of the

RBNZ’s target band.
• Our forecast is a little below the RBNZ’s last
published forecast for 3.9% annual inflation.

We expect the September quarter inflation report (out
on 22 October) will show that New Zealand consumer
prices rose 0.7% over the past three months. That would
see the annual inflation rate slipping back to 3.7%, down
from 4.1% in the year to June. However, as we discuss

in our recent forecast update, that’s likely to be just a
temporary reprieve with the recent rise in oil prices set to
push inflation up again over the coming months.
16 Oct 2026 | Satish Ranchhod, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5668 | +64 21 710 852 | satish.ranchhod@westpac.co.nz
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
CPI preview, September quarter 2026 –
Thursday 22 October, 10:45am.

### Page 2

Westpac New Zealand Economics
Key drivers of quarterly inflation.
Underpinning our forecast for 0.7% inflation in the
September quarter are some big price movements in
both directions.
The largest move in prices this quarter has actually been
a sharp fall in fuel costs. Fuel prices aren’t low by any
stretch of the imagination. However, average prices for
both petrol and diesel over the September quarter were
lower than in the June quarter when prices rocketed

higher in the wake of the Middle East conflict – see the
chart below. In terms of specifics, the average price for 91
unleaded was around 6% lower in September than in the
June quarter, while diesel prices were close to 16% lower.
Fuel spending accounts for around 4% of the CPI, and
these moves have taken 0.4ppts off inflation this quarter.

While prices at the pump weren’t as high as they were in
the June quarter, fuel costs are still painfully high. And
that’s been bleeding through into higher prices for other
goods and services. Of note, the September quarter
saw large increases in passenger transport costs, with
international airfares up 14% and domestic airfares up

nearly 8%. Together those prices account for around 2%
of the CPI.
We also expect building costs (10% of the CPI) will rise by
1.6% this quarter following a similarly chunky rise in June.
Again, much of that rise has been related to higher global
oil prices which have been flowing through to higher
operating and material costs. That’s occurred at the same
time as the amount of planned construction work has
been rising.
The September quarter also sees the annual increases

in local council rates and charges for services like water
supply (around 4% of the CPI). We expect these costs will
be up around 7.4% this year, continuing the run of large
increases seen in previous years.
Lastly, the past few months saw a 1.0% rise in food prices
(18% of the CPI), largely as a result of the seasonal rise in
the price for fresh vegetables.
On the downside, we’ve seen continued softness in
housing rents, which are the largest component of

the CPI, accounting for 10% of the basket. With low
population growth and ample supply, rents have
effectively been flat since the start of this year. In annual
terms, rental inflation is the lowest it’s been in more
than two decades, with regions like Wellington seeing
outright falls.

Where could we be surprised?
There are some key areas where we see the risk
of a downside surprise. The first is the price for
communications equipment, like mobile phones. After
a large rise in the June quarter, we could see a sizeable

drop in prices this quarter.
There could also be softness in used car prices, especially
with high fuel prices encouraging a shift away from
combustion engine vehicles.
However, the risks aren’t all to one side. Demand for more
environmentally friendly new cars has picked up, and that
could push up prices for those vehicles. In addition, we’re
watching to see if high fuel costs are spilling over into
other prices as we’re seeing in sectors like construction.

Core inflation around 3%, not easing.
With big swings in fuel costs and concerns that this is
spilling over into a broader lift in inflation pressures,
the RBNZ will be paying close attention to the various
measures of core inflation (core inflation measures
smooth through quarter-to-quarter swings in individual
prices and instead track the underlying trend in inflation).
We expect that core inflation measures will point to
continued firmness in underlying price pressures, with

several closely watched measures likely to take a step
higher this quarter. In terms of specifics, we expect:
• CPI ex-fuel inflation: +3.0% yr (vs +2.9% previously)
• CPI ex-fuel and food: +3.2% yr (vs +2.9% previously)
• CPI ex-fuel, food and household energy: +2.9% yr (vs
+2.5% previously)

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
We’ll also be watching the distribution of price
changes to see if there’s been a material increase in
the number of prices that are increasing. That could
be an early indication that businesses, even those
with a limited exposure to fuel costs, are starting to
factor in expectations of continued high inflation when
setting prices.
As we’ve noted before, the various measures of core
inflation were not low even before the recent fuel shock.

That means the RBNZ has limited headroom to absorb
upside inflation surprises.
Much of that firmness in core inflation has been due
to increases in domestically oriented non-tradables
prices. We estimate these prices rose by 1.3% over the
September quarter. That would see annual non-tradables
inflation rising to 3.6%, up from 3.4% last quarter.
Non-tradables inflation is a key focus for the RBNZ and
it’s been running at these above-average levels for around

a year now. That’s despite the softness in economic
growth and the labour market. In part, this firmness in
domestic inflation is due to continued large increases in
administered prices, like council rates.
On the imported front, lower fuel costs are expected to
see tradables prices falling by 0.2% over the quarter.
That would result in annual tradables inflation dropping
back to 3.9% from 4.9% previously. Excluding food and
fuel costs, we estimate that tradables prices were up

2.2% over the past year (vs 1.9% in the year to June). That
would be the highest level in three years with the fall in
the NZD boosting import costs.
